5	## Log
6	
7	- Generic parameter names like `input` hide domain meaning. In `messages.edit(thread, input)`, the second argument is not just arbitrary input; it is a message edit carrying `messageId` and `content`. In `messages.deleteMessage(thread, input)`, the second argument is a deletion target carrying `messageId`. The generic name makes implementation easier to stamp out but makes the public surface less readable and less domain-shaped.

33	- Regression tombstones: tests added after deleting, renaming, or refactoring something whose only purpose is to prove the old symbol, old path, or old vocabulary does not spontaneously come back. This is a weird LLM failure mode and appears especially common from GPT-5.5: the model treats a refactor like a thing that needs a grave marker in the test suite instead of testing the live behavior through the current public contract. These tests bloat the suite with fear artifacts. They are only justified when the absence itself is a real contract, such as a migration boundary, compatibility rejection, security rule, or explicit deprecation behavior. Repo root: `/Users/leemoore/code/pi-long-horizon/liminal-context`. Commit: `0ed3749`. Example candidates to watch for during cleanup: renamed message surface operations, retired `form` vocabulary, retired lower-band/projection wording, and removed inference/config paths.
34	
35	- Premature compatibility shims: adding fallback names, old pathways, dual behavior, or backward-compatibility layers during normal early-stage refactors without first asking whether any real client is likely to break. The knee-jerk move is "what if changing this breaks a client" but the missing step is due diligence: is there actually a client, persisted artifact, released API, or migration boundary that needs compatibility? When the answer is no, the shim hides bugs, preserves stale concepts, complicates tests, and adds tech debt exactly where the refactor was trying to simplify the system. Repo root: `/Users/leemoore/code/pi-long-horizon/liminal-context`. Commit: `0ed3749`. Example candidates to watch for during cleanup: message operation renames, derivation terminology cleanup, thread-view domain-boundary refactors, and removed or changed inference assignment paths.
36	
37	- Misplaced mechanical surface on a domain: LHC is an in-process SDK for durable long-horizon conversation context. The desired architecture is domain surfaces for conversation-model concepts (`messages`, `turns`, `thread-view`, `inspect`) over shared technical utilities such as storage, token counting, logging, and the durable work queue. `messages.listQueuedWork` is a concrete bad example: it is exposed on the messages domain, but it returns raw queue rows and speaks queue vocabulary (`workItemId`, owner, kind, source ref, status, queued time). That is not message behavior; it is work-queue mechanics filtered to owner=`messages`. The bad choice is putting a mechanical queue inspection method on a product/domain surface because the queue rows happen to point at message-owned work. This blurs ownership, encourages callers to reason about queue internals through a domain namespace, and makes the messages API less coherent. The right choice is for messages to expose domain-level derivation state and repair operations, such as a message derivation report and requeue-by-derivation target, while shared-tech or an explicit work surface exposes queue inspection with filters like owner, kind, source ref, status, or source version. Repo root: `/Users/leemoore/code/pi-long-horizon/liminal-context`. Commit: `0ed3749`. Example files: `packages/lhc/src/messages/index.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/shared-tech/work-queue/index.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/sdk.ts`.
38	
39	- Generic wrapper drift: taking a clear, specific operation shape and turning it into a vague extensibility container. In this session, the starting point was the bad existing shape `messages.requeue(thread, target)`, which exposed queue mechanics and asked for a derivation target. We refined the desired domain operation to a synchronous message derivation call: derive the message-owned derivation for these message ids. The good shape was `messages.derive(threadRef, messageIds)`, where `messageIds` is a `string[]`; it says exactly what the caller has and lets the messages domain decide what each message can derive. The bad model move was repeatedly trying to turn that into `messages.derive(threadRef, request)` or `messages.derive(threadRef, { messageIds })`. That added a generic wrapper with no current purpose, created wiggle room for future options before a need existed, and obscured the direct domain meaning. This is a common AI coding failure: the model reaches for generic API templates (`input`, `request`, `target`, `opts`) because they are common and feel extensible, but they make early-stage domain surfaces mushier and easier to pollute with unrelated fields later. Repo root: `/Users/leemoore/code/pi-long-horizon/liminal-context`. Commit: `0ed3749`. Example files: `packages/lhc/src/messages/index.ts`; planned cleanup target: replace async/mechanical `messages.requeue` thinking with a direct synchronous `messages.derive(threadRef, messageIds)` domain operation.
40	
41	- Hidden leaky surface naming: keeping a leaky operation exposed but giving it a vague name so the leak is harder to see. In this session, `messages.workHandlers` and `turns.workHandlers` were recognized as queue wiring sitting on domain surfaces. The bad instinct was to soften the leak with a vague noun name like `workQueueHandlers`, which still does not force the caller to confront that this is work-queue registration/wiring, not message or turn behavior. If the leak stays temporarily, naming it something explicit like `registerWorkQHandlers` is better because it names and contains the mess instead of hiding it. Repo root: `/Users/leemoore/code/pi-long-horizon/liminal-context`. Commit: `0ed3749`. Example files: `packages/lhc/src/messages/index.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/turns/index.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/sdk.ts`.
42	
43	- Vague noun bucket operation names: exports named as bags of things instead of operations or clear wiring roles. `workHandlers` and the proposed `sdkWorkHandlers` are bad names because they do not say whether the code registers, assembles, dispatches, or owns anything. They become buckets where unrelated behavior can accumulate because the name has no verb and no boundary. In the work-queue discussion, this showed up when trying to explain why `messages.workHandlers` exists: the caller has to inspect SDK construction to learn that it is handler registration material for the durable queue. Repo root: `/Users/leemoore/code/pi-long-horizon/liminal-context`. Commit: `0ed3749`. Example files: `packages/lhc/src/messages/index.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/turns/index.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/sdk.ts`.
44	
45	- Inventing a new top-level bucket to hide cross-domain wiring: moving a boundary problem out of one surface by creating a new top-level internal module before deciding where the responsibility belongs. In this session, one proposed fix for `messages.workHandlers` was a new `sdk-work-handlers` / `sdkWorkHandlers` module. That would remove the leak from `messages`, but it risks creating a special SDK composition bucket that imports across domains and shared-tech without settling whether durable work registration belongs to shared-tech, SDK construction, or explicit domain registration. This can turn into a new accumulation point for cross-domain glue. Repo root: `/Users/leemoore/code/pi-long-horizon/liminal-context`. Commit: `0ed3749`. Example files: `packages/lhc/src/sdk.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/messages/internal/handlers.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/turns/internal/handlers.ts`.
46	
47	- Overriding a precise user-chosen name with a softened generic alternative: a model failure where the user proposes a concrete name that exposes the actual concern, and the model rewrites it back into neutral abstraction. In this session, the user proposed `messages.registerWorkQHandlers` to make exposed queue wiring obvious. The model changed it to `workQueueHandlers`, which hid the procedural registration/wiring concern behind another noun bucket. The failure is not just naming taste; it removes diagnostic value from the name and makes a known architectural mess less visible to future readers. Repo root: `/Users/leemoore/code/pi-long-horizon/liminal-context`. Commit: `0ed3749`. Example files: `packages/lhc/src/messages/index.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/turns/index.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/sdk.ts`.
48	
49	- Misplaced construction wiring on runtime domain surfaces: exporting SDK/work-queue initialization material from functional domains as if it were part of their runtime API. `messages.workHandlers` and `turns.workHandlers` are used by SDK construction to build the durable work handler map; product callers do not use them as message or turn operations. The handler implementations are domain-owned, but the composition step belongs to technical infrastructure. Putting that wiring on `messages` and `turns` makes the domain surfaces carry construction concerns, blurs what a caller should use, and encourages future initialization mechanics to leak into functional APIs. The better shape is for domain internals to keep their handler implementations and for shared-tech/work-queue to expose a technical operation such as `mapWorkQHandlers(handlerGroups)` that SDK initialization calls. Repo root: `/Users/leemoore/code/pi-long-horizon/liminal-context`. Commit: `0ed3749`. Example files: `packages/lhc/src/messages/index.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/turns/index.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/sdk.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/messages/internal/handlers.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/turns/internal/handlers.ts`.
50	
51	- Isolated permutation tests that bypass the real entry point: adding or exposing a low-level pure function just so tests can enumerate state permutations instead of exercising the service operation that owns the real contract. In this repo, `turns.transition` was exported from the turns surface and tested by `packages/lhc/test/state-machine.test.ts` as a pure rule table: every event kind in no-open-turn and open-turn states. That looks exhaustive, but it avoids the harder and higher-signal work of sending real batches through `intakeStream.messageEvents` and predicting the actual durable results: event order, message ids, turn transitions, message membership, queued turn work, skipped-event behavior, and rollback behavior. The failure mode is coverage inflation with reduced signal. The test proves a local permutation table, but it does not prove the broader path where invalid states are filtered, membership is stamped, messages are created, turns are opened/closed, queue rows are written, and reads return the expected durable state. This pattern can be valid for code with many highly specified state permutations where the state machine itself is a real public/product contract or where service-level scenarios cannot reasonably cover important states. That is not this case. Here the valid states are best reached through the normal intake surface. The better test shape is to drive `intakeStream.messageEvents`, then assert the returned `turnTransitions`, `messages.list` membership, `turns.listTurns` records, and queued turn work only where a real turn close occurred. That forces the test author to understand the data entering the front door and the exact states the application should reach, instead of bypassing the integrated path and getting a false sense of safety from isolated permutations. Repo root: `/Users/leemoore/code/pi-long-horizon/liminal-context`. Commit: `866aba1`. Example files: `packages/lhc/src/turns/index.ts`, `packages/lhc/src/turns/internal/state-machine.ts`, `packages/lhc/test/state-machine.test.ts`, `packages/lhc/test/turns.test.ts`.
52	
53	- Special code whose only purpose is to isolate internal machinery for tests is usually a smell, especially when the thing being isolated is not the real contract the system cares about. In this repo, durable-work/queue tests originally pushed on the runtime SDK surface itself by exposing `sdk.workHandlers`, `sdk.workDispatchers`, and `sdk.lookupWorkHandler(...)` so fixtures could inject fake handler maps. That is bad because it teaches the product API about queue wiring purely for test convenience, and it steers tests toward mutating internal dispatch machinery instead of exercising the real entry points (`enqueue`, `drain`, domain `derive*`, intake, and thread-view sweep). A follow-on test-only seam like `registerTestingWork(...)` is less bad than leaking those fields on the runtime API, but it is still evidence that the tests want to isolate the wrong thing. The broader failure mode is building custom testing hooks so you can directly poke internals that should stay internal, instead of writing higher-signal tests around the actual operational seams. The better direction is: keep any unavoidable helper close to the technical module being tested, keep it out of runtime product surfaces, and prefer tests that drive the real queue/derive/drain flow and assert durable outcomes. Repo root: `/Users/leemoore/code/pi-long-horizon/liminal-context`. Current example files: `packages/lhc/src/sdk.ts`, `packages/lhc/test/fixtures/work-handlers.ts`, `packages/lhc/test/work-queue.test.ts`.

[tool result · toolu_01TG6rnA8wr5ekFitf6zMe9d]
1	# Backlog
2	
3	Fixes, tighten-ups, and feature work on the road to v1.
4	
5	
6	
7	
8	## Architecture
9	
10	### 35. SDK hygiene slice from the July code review
11	
12	**Problem:** A code-review pass over the SDK core found the version-fenced work-completion logic — the most correctness-critical code in the system — existing in two copies: one live, one dead with zero call sites, and the copies have already drifted. Three smaller findings ride along: the write-transaction helper leaves error-wrapping to caller convention instead of guaranteeing it; compact stamps its snapshot timestamp from the wall clock instead of the injectable clock seam prune uses; and compact's isolation from concurrent intake is logical rather than lock-level, which deserves a comment so future readers don't assume SQLite serializes it.
13	
14	**Solution:** One small slice: delete the dead completion/failure duplicate from the work queue; centralize error wrapping inside the transaction helper so infra failures always surface through the result contract; thread the clock seam into compact; add the isolation comment.
15	
16	**Status:** Not started. All four verified against code 2026-07-10; the dead copy is the only correctness hazard, the rest are riders.
17	
18	
19	### 34. Thread manager + viewer
20	
21	**Problem:** Finding and selecting a thread to resume currently requires manual registry spelunking — there is no unified interface for browsing threads and their views.
22	
23	**Solution:** A thread manager and viewer interface, legible to both agents and humans, accessible from any host (the PI extension, the Claude Code wrapper, the Codex wrapper, and future hosts) for selecting threads or thread views to resume.
24	
25	**Status:** Not started. Scoped as a small feature epic. Origin: Lee, 2026-07-10.
26	
27	
28	### 17. SDK-owned native inference (default), host callbacks demoted
29	
30	**Problem:** Every mystery during the derivation-tuning rounds lived below the host's inference callback boundary — invisible system-prompt drops, instruction wrapping by host harnesses. The callback interface is the load-bearing inference path, but it's opaque: what actually reaches the model is unknowable from the SDK side.
31	
32	**Solution:** The SDK ships its own direct HTTP inference client (targeting the standard chat-completions API shape) as the default derivation lane — wire-observable, with explicit reasoning-effort control and wire-truth logging. Host inference callbacks stay as a substitution point for constrained environments (e.g., enterprise seats where the host's CLI is the only model access), not the primary path. One serving path across all hosts means prompts tune once. The wrapper hosts (Claude Code, Codex) have no host model registry to bridge anyway.
33	
34	**Status:** Not started. The SDK currently uses inference callbacks exclusively, through a four-operation boundary (smoothing, tool-result summarization, turn compression, chunk brief).
35	
36	### 18. PI-extension rework: named compact profiles + tech-debt cleanup + inference re-plumb
37	
38	**Problem:** The PI extension has accumulated more tech debt than the SDK. Three things need a single-pass rework: (a) the extension hardcodes a single compact profile instead of selecting from the SDK's named profiles; (b) general connector code needs cleanup (handler shape, serving, lifecycle); (c) the inference bridge routes through the PI model registry, which introduces host-specific quirks into the derivation path.
39	
40	**Solution:** One pass: (a) wire the extension to select from the SDK's built-in named compact profiles (continuation, conversation, coding) — the SDK side is already built with validation and user-profile merging; what remains is the connector side, which still selects a hardcoded profile with fixed constants; (b) clean up the connector code; (c) re-plumb inference to use the SDK's native lane (item 17) instead of the PI model registry — the registry goes back to serving only the agent.
41	
42	**Status:** (a) is half done — the SDK side is built, but the extension still passes the hardcoded profile. (b) and (c) are not started.
43	
44	### 21. Inference provider/model-selection layer (scoped deliberately)
45	
46	**Problem:** The provider layer for item 17's native inference. Provider/model selection always looks easy and becomes a headache as permutations fan out — this is one of the genuinely hard parts of this space: not cognitive intricacy, but finding the right balance of inference options offered flexibly yet constrained.
47	
48	**Solution:** Chat-completions API only — single-turn, no streaming, no tools. Providers: OpenRouter, Cerebras, a Claude Code CLI subprocess fallback, a ChatGPT OAuth path, and possibly a GLM API. Within a provider: a curated model subset and a set of common thinking-level options — selectable enums mapped to each provider's model string and settings, handling provider quirks. The PI agent's existing provider implementations are starting points.
49	
50	**Status:** Not started. Sequencing: OpenRouter alone until basics are dialed. Hard dependency: a high-speed inexpensive provider (Cerebras) must be in place before any derivation that fires on a large percentage of message events (item 11's bar: provably drains at intake speed).
51	
52	
53	
54	### 24. Post-brief horizon compression (level-2 chunks)
55	
56	**Problem:** Content starts falling out of the brief band after roughly 1–2 weeks of real usage — beyond that horizon, turns have no representation at all.
57	
58	**Solution:** A compression tier past brief: compact chunks into something coarser (chunks of chunks), so the oldest end degrades to a very cheap outcome-level narration instead of dropping out entirely. Extends the band gradient's degrade-over-drop principle to the whole thread lifetime.
59	
60	**Status:** Not started. No level-2 chunk concept exists in the SDK.
61	
62	### 11. Tool-call/result derivation refinement (truncation holds the line)
63	
64	**Problem:** Tool calls and results are truncated to 500 characters as an interim measure. The inference-backed summarization path is fully built and wired — a content classifier routes tool results by operation class, response shape, and prompt mode — but it was forced off because inference clogged the work queue when it fired on every tool result at intake rate.
65	
66	**Solution:** Three stages: (1) study a headless open-source project's deterministic tool-output processing to get more done without inference, reserving model calls for key leverage points; (2) re-enable and validate the classifier-routed inference path (the classifier exists and is wired in, just bypassed by a force-fallback flag — this is re-enable-and-validate, not rebuild); (3) evaluate inference for tool activity only if both quality and speed prove out. The bar: provably drains at intake speed. The Cerebras exploration (item 19) doubles as the feasibility probe.
67	
68	**Status:** 500-character truncation is the production behavior. The classifier, inference plumbing, and prompt template are all built and wired — switching off the force-fallback flag would re-enable them. Blocked on a high-speed inference lane that can keep up with intake-rate bursts.
69	
70	
71	### 27. Thread clone + fork via file copy, with byte-exact reload fidelity
72	
73	**Problem:** Forking a thread is expensive because the current path replays events rather than copying the underlying file. A 300k-token thread costs proportionally more to fork than a 3k one, and replay doesn't carry derivations — the fork re-derives the entire cascade.
74	
75	**Solution:** Since each thread is a single file, clone is a file copy: copy the file, stamp a new thread id in the copy's metadata, insert a registry row. The clone carries all derivations (no re-inference), and cost is constant regardless of thread size. Fork-from-earlier-point is the harder variant: truncate everything past the fork point plus all downstream material as an SDK operation. The PI extension follows with a clone command riding the SDK operation.
76	
77	Clone-as-subagent: run a cloned thread as a subagent, seeded from the clone's view, getting the warm prompt-cache benefit of sharing the source thread's entire served prefix. Hard dependency: byte-exact reload fidelity must be locked first — the cache benefit only exists if the clone's served context is byte-identical to the source's live prefix.
78	
79	Reload fidelity: the served context after a reload/rehydrate/fork-seed must be byte-identical to what the live in-memory session was serving. Suspicion on record: subtle differences on reload (whitespace, ordering, re-rendering drift) silently break provider prompt-cache prefixes, turning a warm cache into a full-price re-read. Verification: capture the live session's rendered context, reload/clone, capture again, diff to the character — any divergence is a defect. The PI extension has an existing replay verification seam closest to this.
80	
81	**Status:** Not started as an SDK operation. The PI extension has an existing replay-based fork path (separate from this design) and an existing replay verification module.
82	
83	### 26. Host-environment cleanup: config directory + key-file consolidation
84	
85	**Problem:** Two housekeeping debts from wiring the PI extensions: (a) the PI extension didn't have its own config directory — it rode the global PI and LHC directories; (b) API keys were split across two locations — the PI agent's auth file and the LHC environment file.
86	
87	**Solution (shipped):** Unify into one home (`PI_LHC_HOME` → `~/.pi-lhc`): `home.ts` module (`registry.sqlite`, `threads/`, `pi/agent/` via `PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR`), offline migration (`scripts/migrate-to-pi-lhc.mjs`) + backup rail (`scripts/pi-lhc-backup.sh`), and fresh-install setup (`.setup/pi-lhc-standalone.md`). 214	function unknownWorkKind(kind: string): { ok: false; error: ErrorResult } {
215	  return {
216	    ok: false,
217	    error: {
218	      errorClass: "state_corruption",
219	      code: "unknown_work_kind",
220	      reason: `no handler registered for work kind "${kind}"`,
221	    },
222	  };
223	}
224	
225	// Dispatch-time lookup: an unregistered kind is reported explicitly — never
226	// a throw, never a silent undefined.
227	export function lookupWorkHandler(map: WorkHandlerMap, kind: string): OpResult<WorkHandler> {
228	  const handler = map[kind as WorkKind];
229	  if (handler === undefined) return unknownWorkKind(kind);
230	  return { ok: true, value: handler };
231	}
232	
233	export function lookupWorkDispatcher(
234	  map: DurableWorkDispatcherMap,
235	  operation: DurableWorkOperation | undefined,
236	  kind: string,
237	): OpResult<DurableWorkDispatcher> {
238	  if (operation === undefined) return unknownWorkKind(kind);
239	  const dispatcher = map[operation.operation];
240	  if (dispatcher === undefined) return unknownWorkKind(kind);
241	  return { ok: true, value: dispatcher };
242	}

307	interface WorkRegistration {
308	  workHandlers: WorkHandlerMap;
309	  workDispatchers: DurableWorkDispatcherMap;
310	}

312	const workRegistrationBySdk = new WeakMap<Lhc, WorkRegistration>();
313	
314	export function registerTestingWork(
315	  sdk: Lhc,
316	  registration: { handlers?: WorkHandlerMap; dispatchers?: DurableWorkDispatcherMap },
317	): void {
318	  const target = workRegistrationBySdk.get(sdk);
319	  if (target === undefined) {
320	    throw new TypeError("registerTestingWork called with an SDK not created by initLhc");
321	  }
322	  if (registration.handlers !== undefined) Object.assign(target.workHandlers, registration.handlers);
323	  if (registration.dispatchers !== undefined) Object.assign(target.workDispatchers, registration.dispatchers);
324	}

326	const INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX = "initLhc config";
327	
328	function requirePositive(value: number, name: string): void {
329	  if (!Number.isFinite(value) || value <= 0) {
330	    throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: ${name} must be a positive number, got ${value}`);
331	  }
332	}
333	
334	function requireNonNegative(value: number, name: string): void {
335	  if (!Number.isFinite(value) || value < 0) {
336	    throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: ${name} must be a non-negative number, got ${value}`);
337	  }
338	}

340	// Bind a domain surface to one SDK instance's delivery seam (epic-fix-001):
341	// every operation invoked through sdk.* runs inside runWithInstanceSeam, so
342	// the poke/touch it triggers deep inside reaches THIS SDK's scheduler
343	// (background) or a no-op (manual) — never another SDK's. Non-function exports
344	// pass through unchanged. The wrapped object is the same shape as the
345	// namespace, so the public surface type holds.
346	function scopeSurface<T extends object>(surface: T, seam: InstanceSeam): T {
347	  const scoped: Record<string, unknown> = {};
348	  for (const key of Object.keys(surface)) {
349	    const value = (surface as Record<string, unknown>)[key];
350	    scoped[key] =
351	      typeof value === "function"
352	        ? (...args: unknown[]): unknown =>
353	            runWithInstanceSeam(seam, () => (value as (...a: unknown[]) => unknown)(...args))
354	        : value;
355	  }
356	  return scoped as T;
357	}

359	// The default provider lane and model for inference derivation types, used
360	// when the host omits an inference type from inference.assignments. The
361	// provider key is a host routing key; LHC never resolves it.
362	const DEFAULT_INFERENCE_LANE = { provider: "codex", model: "gpt-5.4-mini" } as const;
363	
364	// Default assignment per inference derivation type: documented default lane and
365	// model, registry default prompt template, and tested target ratios for
366	// compression/brief types. Deterministic derivations are not inference
367	// assignments. Construction-internal: defaults are observable through routed
368	// calls, not part of the public export surface.
369	const DEFAULT_INFERENCE_THINKING = "none" as const;
370	
371	const DEFAULT_INFERENCE_ASSIGNMENTS: Readonly<Record<string, ModelAssignment>> = {
372	  smoothed_prompt: {
373	    provider: DEFAULT_INFERENCE_LANE.provider,
374	    model: DEFAULT_INFERENCE_LANE.model,
375	    prompt: DEFAULT_PROMPT_NAMES.smoothed_prompt ?? "smoothing-v1",
376	    thinking: DEFAULT_INFERENCE_THINKING,
377	  },
378	  tool_result_summary: {
379	    provider: DEFAULT_INFERENCE_LANE.provider,
380	    model: DEFAULT_INFERENCE_LANE.model,
381	    prompt: DEFAULT_PROMPT_NAMES.tool_result_summary ?? "tool-result-v2",
382	    thinking: DEFAULT_INFERENCE_THINKING,
383	  },
384	  detailed_turn_compression: {
385	    provider: DEFAULT_INFERENCE_LANE.provider,
386	    model: DEFAULT_INFERENCE_LANE.model,
387	    // Stated prompt target (v3) is lower than these ratios — see detailed-turn-compression-v3.ts.
388	    prompt: DEFAULT_PROMPT_NAMES.detailed_turn_compression ?? "detailed-turn-compression-v3",
389	    // These ratios (and the targets stated in prompts) are steering, not decided
390	    // specs or tolerances. Ballparks that get the model in the zone; adjust freely.
391	    targetMinRatio: 0.35,
392	    targetMaxRatio: 0.65,
393	    targetAimRatio: 0.5,
394	    thinking: DEFAULT_INFERENCE_THINKING,
395	  },
396	  chunk_summary_brief: {
397	    provider: DEFAULT_INFERENCE_LANE.provider,
398	    model: DEFAULT_INFERENCE_LANE.model,
399	    // Stated prompt target (v3) is lower than these ratios — see chunk-brief-v3.ts.
400	    prompt: DEFAULT_PROMPT_NAMES.chunk_summary_brief ?? "chunk-brief-v3",
401	    // Steering, not specs — see the comment on detailed_turn_compression above.
402	    targetMinRatio: 0.08,
403	    targetMaxRatio: 0.2,
404	    targetAimRatio: 0.12,
405	    thinking: DEFAULT_INFERENCE_THINKING,
406	  },
407	};
408	
409	function resolveTargetRatios(
410	  kind: "detailed_turn_compression" | "chunk_summary_brief",
411	  assignment?: ModelAssignment,
412	): ResolvedSdkConfig["compressionTargets"] {
413	  const defaults = DEFAULT_INFERENCE_ASSIGNMENTS[kind]!;
414	  return {
415	    minRatio: assignment?.targetMinRatio ?? defaults.targetMinRatio!,
416	    aimRatio: assignment?.targetAimRatio ?? defaults.targetAimRatio!,
417	    maxRatio: assignment?.targetMaxRatio ?? defaults.targetMaxRatio!,
418	  };
419	}
420	
421	// Resolve the `inference` construction path: validate the host function and
422	// assignment map, then fill defaults. Provided inference assignments must carry
423	// non-empty provider/model and a registry-known prompt. Inference types the host
424	// omits are filled from DEFAULT_INFERENCE_ASSIGNMENTS. Unknown keys are
425	// rejected, never silently ignored. Then the adapter is built into the same
426	// InferenceCallbacks slot direct injection uses. No partial construction: every
427	// mistake throws before anything is assembled.
428	function resolveInferenceCallbacks(
429	  inference: InferenceConfig,
430	  guards: ResolvedSdkConfig["guards"],
431	): InferenceCallbacks {
432	  if (typeof inference.call !== "function") {
433	    throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: inference.call must be a function`);
434	  }
435	  const provided = inference.assignments ?? {};
436	  if (provided === null || typeof provided !== "object") {
437	    throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: inference.assignments must be an object`);
438	  }
439	
440	  const inferenceKeys = new Set<string>(Object.keys(DEFAULT_INFERENCE_ASSIGNMENTS));
441	
442	  // Unknown keys are rejected (anti-shim: never silently ignore) — TC-0.3a.
443	  for (const key of Object.keys(provided)) {
444	    if (!inferenceKeys.has(key)) {
445	      throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: inference.assignments has unknown derivation type "${key}"`);
446	    }
447	  }
448	
449	  // Validate every effective inference assignment after merging defaults: non-empty
450	  // provider/model/prompt, and prompt must name a registry template.
451	  const merged: Record<string, ModelAssignment> = {};
452	  for (const kind of inferenceKeys) {
453	    const override = provided[kind];
454	    const assignment: ModelAssignment =
455	      override === undefined
456	        ? { ...DEFAULT_INFERENCE_ASSIGNMENTS[kind]! }
457	        : { ...DEFAULT_INFERENCE_ASSIGNMENTS[kind]!, ...override };
458	    if (assignment === null || typeof assignment !== "object") {
459	      throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: inference.assignments.${kind} must be an object`);
460	    }
461	    for (const field of ["provider", "model", "prompt"] as const) {
462	      const value = assignment[field];
463	      if (typeof value !== "string" || value.trim() === "") {
464	        throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: inference.assignments.${kind}.${field} must be a non-empty string`);
465	      }
466	    }
467	    for (const field of ["targetMinRatio", "targetAimRatio", "targetMaxRatio"] as const) {
468	      const value = assignment[field];
469	      if (value !== undefined && (!Number.isFinite(value) || value <= 0)) {
470	        throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: inference.assignments.${kind}.${field} must be a positive number`);
471	      }
472	    }
473	    if (PROMPT_REGISTRY[assignment.prompt] === undefined) {
474	      throw new TypeError(
475	        `${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: inference.assignments.${kind}.prompt names unknown template "${assignment.prompt}"`,
476	      );
477	    }
478	    merged[kind] = assignment;
479	  }
480	
481	  const timeoutMs = inference.timeoutMs ?? 60_000;
482	  const maxInputChars = inference.maxInputChars ?? 200_000;
483	  requirePositive(timeoutMs, "inference.timeoutMs");
484	  requirePositive(maxInputChars, "inference.maxInputChars");
485	  return createInferenceCallbacks({
486	    call: inference.call,
487	    assignments: merged,
488	    guards,
489	    timeoutMs,
490	    maxInputChars,
491	  });
492	}
493	
494	// The only initialization path: inference callbacks, mode, clock, and policy enter here.
495	// Config mistakes are programmer errors at construction and throw; operating
496	// failures after construction return OpResults per the error contract.
497	export function initLhc(config: SdkConfig): Lhc {
498	  // Inference callbacks arrive by direct injection or by the inference config
499	  // There is no named inference-callback registry and no env/flag resolution
500	  // path to fall back on. The XOR rule is validated before anything downstream
501	  // so the error names the caller's mistake, not a symptom.
502	  if (config.inferenceCallbacks !== undefined && config.inference !== undefined) {
503	    throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: exactly one of inferenceCallbacks or inference`);
504	  }
505	  if (config.inferenceCallbacks === undefined && config.inference === undefined) {
506	    throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: exactly one of inferenceCallbacks or inference`);
507	  }
508	  if (config.mode !== "background" && config.mode !== "manual") {
509	    throw new TypeError(
510	      `${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: mode must be "background" or "manual", got ${JSON.stringify(config.mode)}`,
511	    );
512	  }
513	  const guards = resolveGuards(config.guards);
514	  const compressionTargets = resolveTargetRatios(
515	    "detailed_turn_compression",
516	    config.inference?.assignments?.detailed_turn_compression,
517	  );
518	  const briefTargets = resolveTargetRatios("chunk_summary_brief", config.inference?.assignments?.chunk_summary_brief);
519	
520	  let inferenceCallbacks: InferenceCallbacks;
521	  if (config.inference !== undefined) {
522	    inferenceCallbacks = resolveInferenceCallbacks(config.inference, guards);
523	  } else {
524	    const directCallbacks = config.inferenceCallbacks;
525	    if (directCallbacks === null || typeof directCallbacks !== "object") {
526	      throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: inferenceCallbacks must implement InferenceCallbacks`);
527	    }
528	    for (const operation of INFERENCE_CALLBACK_OPERATIONS) {
529	      if (typeof directCallbacks[operation] !== "function") {
530	        throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: inferenceCallbacks is missing operation ${operation}`);
531	      }
532	    }
533	    inferenceCallbacks = directCallbacks;
534	  }
535	
536	  const resolved: ResolvedSdkConfig = {
537	    inferenceCallbacks,
538	    mode: config.mode,
539	    clock: config.clock ?? (() => new Date()),
540	    retry: config.retry ?? { budget: 3, backoffBaseMs: 5000, backoffCapMs: 60000 },
541	    guards,
542	    compressionTargets,
543	    briefTargets,
544	    toolResult: config.toolResult ?? {
545	      smallTierTokens: 1000,
546	      smallTargetRatio: 0.15,
547	      midTargetRatio: 0.04,
548	    },
549	    lease: config.lease ?? { durationMs: 120000 },
550	    chunkPolicy: config.chunkPolicy ?? { targetProjectedTokens: 2200, maxProjectedTokens: 4400 },
551	    // Built-ins merged with user profiles by name; band sums and visibility
552	    // budgets validated, throwing with the violated setting named.
553	    view: resolveViewConfig(config.view),
554	  };
555	  requirePositive(resolved.retry.budget, "retry.budget");
556	  requireNonNegative(resolved.retry.backoffBaseMs, "retry.backoffBaseMs");
557	  requireNonNegative(resolved.retry.backoffCapMs, "retry.backoffCapMs");
558	  if (resolved.retry.backoffCapMs < resolved.retry.backoffBaseMs) {
559	    throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: retry.backoffCapMs must be >= retry.backoffBaseMs`);
560	  }

562	  requirePositive(resolved.guards.smoothedPrompt.suspiciousOutputRatio, "guards.smoothedPrompt.suspiciousOutputRatio");
563	  requirePositive(resolved.guards.toolResultSummary.timeoutMs, "guards.toolResultSummary.timeoutMs");
564	  requirePositive(
565	    resolved.guards.detailedTurnCompression.tinyTurnTokens,
566	    "guards.detailedTurnCompression.tinyTurnTokens",
567	  );
568	  requirePositive(resolved.compressionTargets.minRatio, "compressionTargets.minRatio");
569	  requirePositive(resolved.compressionTargets.aimRatio, "compressionTargets.aimRatio");
570	  requirePositive(resolved.compressionTargets.maxRatio, "compressionTargets.maxRatio");
571	  if (resolved.compressionTargets.maxRatio < resolved.compressionTargets.minRatio) {
572	    throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: compressionTargets.maxRatio must be >= minRatio`);

581	  requirePositive(resolved.briefTargets.aimRatio, "briefTargets.aimRatio");

588	    resolved.briefTargets.aimRatio > resolved.briefTargets.maxRatio

591	  }

593	  requirePositive(resolved.toolResult.smallTargetRatio, "toolResult.smallTargetRatio");
594	  requirePositive(resolved.toolResult.midTargetRatio, "toolResult.midTargetRatio");
595	  requirePositive(resolved.lease.durationMs, "lease.durationMs");
596	  requirePositive(resolved.chunkPolicy.targetProjectedTokens, "chunkPolicy.targetProjectedTokens");
597	  if (resolved.chunkPolicy.maxProjectedTokens < resolved.chunkPolicy.targetProjectedTokens) {
598	    throw new TypeError(`${INIT_CONFIG_PREFIX}: chunkPolicy.maxProjectedTokens must be >= targetProjectedTokens`);
599	  }
600	
601	  // Handler maps merge from per-domain contributions at construction.
602	  const workHandlers = mapWorkQHandlers([messageWorkHandlers, turnWorkHandlers]);
603	  const workDispatchers: DurableWorkDispatcherMap = {
604	    "messages.derive": (run, item) => dispatchMessageDeriveWork(run, item),
605	    "turns.deriveTurn": (run, item) => dispatchTurnOwnedWork(run, { ...item, kind: "turn_derivation" }),
606	    "turns.deriveDetailedTurnCompression": (run, item) =>
607	      dispatchTurnOwnedWork(run, { ...item, kind: "detailed_turn_compression" }),
608	    "turns.deriveDetailedChunk": (run, item) => dispatchTurnOwnedWork(run, { ...item, kind: "chunk_summary_detailed" }),
609	    "turns.deriveBriefChunk": (run, item) => dispatchTurnOwnedWork(run, { ...item, kind: "chunk_summary_brief" }),
610	  };
611	
612	  const drainDeps: DrainDeps = {
613	    lookupDispatcher: (operation, kind) => lookupWorkDispatcher(workDispatchers, operation, kind),
614	    hasAnyHandler: () => Object.keys(workDispatchers).length > 0,
615	    config: resolved,
616	    openThreadDatabase: threadsDomain.openThreadDatabase,
617	  };
618	  const scheduler = createScheduler(resolved.mode, drainDeps);
619	
620	  // This SDK's per-instance delivery seam. Every operation
621	  // invoked through the scoped surfaces below runs inside it, so enqueue pokes
622	  // and thread-file touches reach this instance's scheduler in background mode,
623	  // or a no-op in manual mode, isolated from any other SDK alive in the process.
624	  // A manual SDK therefore never auto-drains, whatever the construction order.
625	  // The seam also carries this instance's resolved view config: thread-view
626	  // operations invoked through sdk.* read this SDK's profiles/budgets/threshold;
627	  // below-SDK direct domain calls fall back to built-in defaults inside the
628	  // thread-view surface.
629	  const seam: InstanceSeam =
630	    resolved.mode === "background"
631	      ? {
632	          poke: (threadId) => scheduler.poke(threadId),
633	          touch: (filePath, db) => scheduler.touch(filePath, db),
634	          view: resolved.view,
635	          config: resolved,
636	        }
637	      : { poke: () => {}, touch: () => {}, view: resolved.view, config: resolved };
638	
639	  // Background mode also installs the below-SDK default seam so a direct
640	  // domain call made with no SDK scope — a top-level mutation in the
641	  // single-background default path — still reaches the one installed
642	  // scheduler. The per-instance scoping above overrides this for every sdk.*
643	  // call, so the default can never auto-drain a manual SDK's work. Manual mode
644	  // leaves the default alone (pokes stay no-ops by contract).
645	  if (resolved.mode === "background") {
646	    installSchedulerPoke((threadId) => scheduler.poke(threadId));
647	    setThreadTouch((filePath, db) => scheduler.touch(filePath, db));
648	  }
649	
650	  const work: WorkSurface = {
651	    drain: (ref, opts) =>
652	      runWithInstanceSeam(seam, async () => {
653	        const resolvedRef = await threadsDomain.resolveThreadRef(ref);
654	        if (!resolvedRef.ok) return resolvedRef;
655	        return runDrain(resolvedRef.value.filePath, drainDeps, opts);
656	      }),
657	  };
658	
659	  const logging: LoggingSurface = {
660	    write: (ref, entry) =>
661	      runWithInstanceSeam(seam, async () => {
662	        try {
663	          const written = await createDbWriteTransaction(
664	            ref,
665	            (transaction) => {
666	              loggingDomain.writeLog(transaction, entry);
667	            },
668	            resolved.clock,
669	          );
670	          return written.ok ? { ok: true as const, value: undefined } : written;
671	        } catch (cause) {
672	          const reason = cause instanceof Error ? cause.message : String(cause);
673	          return storageFailure(`log write failed: ${reason}`);
674	        }
675	      }),
676	    query: (ref, q) =>
677	      runWithInstanceSeam(seam, async () => {
678	        try {
679	          return await createDbReadTransaction(ref, (transaction) => loggingDomain.queryLog(transaction.db, q));
680	        } catch (cause) {
681	          const reason = cause instanceof Error ? cause.message : String(cause);
682	          return storageFailure(`log query failed: ${reason}`);
683	        }
684	      }),
685	    queryDerivationLog: (ref, q) =>
686	      runWithInstanceSeam(seam, async () => {
687	        try {
688	          return await createDbReadTransaction(ref, (transaction) =>
689	            loggingDomain.queryDerivationLog(transaction.db, q),
690	          );
691	        } catch (cause) {
692	          const reason = cause instanceof Error ? cause.message : String(cause);
693	          return storageFailure(`derivation log query failed: ${reason}`);
694	        }
695	      }),
696	  };
697	
698	  const intakeStreamSurface: IntakeStreamSurface = {
699	    ...intakeStreamDomain,
700	    initLhc,
701	  };
702	
703	  const sdk: Lhc = {
704	    threads: threadsDomain,
705	    intakeStream: scopeSurface(intakeStreamSurface, seam),
706	    messages: scopeSurface(messagesDomain, seam),
707	    turns: scopeSurface(turnsDomain, seam),
708	    threadView: scopeSurface<ThreadViewSurface>(
709	      {
710	        getLlmRequestContext: threadViewDomain.getLlmRequestContext,
711	        getSessionThreadView: threadViewDomain.getSessionThreadView,
712	        status: threadViewDomain.status,
713	        prune: threadViewDomain.prune,
714	        describe: threadViewDomain.describe,
715	        previewCompact: threadViewDomain.previewCompact,
716	        compact: threadViewDomain.compact,
717	        materialize: threadViewDomain.materialize,
718	      },
719	      seam,
720	    ),
721	    inspect: scopeSurface(inspectDomain, seam),
722	    logging,
723	    config: resolved,
724	    scheduler,
725	    work,
726	    drainSettled: async (ref) => {
727	      const resolvedRef = await threadsDomain.resolveThreadRef(ref);
728	      if (!resolvedRef.ok) return; // nothing can be scheduled for an unresolvable ref
729	      const threadId = peekThreadId(resolvedRef.value.filePath);
730	      if (threadId === null) return;
731	      return scheduler.drainSettled(threadId);
732	    },
733	  };
734	  workRegistrationBySdk.set(sdk, { workHandlers, workDispatchers });
735	  return sdk;
736	}

=== The untracked freeze-repro test (why?) ===
82 packages/cc-lhc/test/wrapper/freeze-repro.test.ts
// Originally an investigation repro for two modal freezes; kept as the
// regression record now that both are fixed:
//
//  1. kitty-encoded ctrl-C (CSI 99;5u) was dropped while a command executed.
//     claude pushes kitty disambiguate mode, so kitty-protocol terminals
//     (iTerm2, Warp, Ghostty) never send raw 0x03 — leaving NO working
//     detach key: Esc dropped by design, leader dropped by design, ctrl-C
//     dropped by omission. Now every cancel-family key detaches in every
//     encoding.
//
//  2. a stray string-terminator introducer (ESC ] — alt-], or a truncated
//     OSC response) wedged the modal permanently: every later keypress
//     forwarded to the pty as presumed protocol. Raw ctrl-C / the leader
//     cannot occur inside a legal string payload, so they now escape the
//     wedge (closing the child's dangling introducer with ST).

import { describe, expect, it } from "vitest";

import { createInputState, processInputChunk, showReceipts } from "../../src/wrapper/modal.js";

const LEADER_KITTY = Buffer.from("\x1b[93;5u");
const ESC_KITTY = Buffer.from("\x1b[27u");
const ENTER_KITTY = Buffer.from("\x1b[13u");
const CTRL_C_KITTY = Buffer.from("\x1b[99;5u");

describe("modal freeze regressions (kitty encodings)", () => {
  it("kitty ctrl-C detaches an executing command, same as raw 0x03", () => {
    // Enter modal via kitty leader, type status, kitty Enter -> executing
    let r = processInputChunk(LEADER_KITTY, createInputState());
    expect(r.actions).toEqual([{ kind: "enter_modal" }]);
    r = processInputChunk(Buffer.from("status"), r.state);
    r = processInputChunk(ENTER_KITTY, r.state);
    expect(r.actions).toEqual([{ kind: "execute", commandLine: "/lhc-status" }]);
    expect(r.state.mode).toBe("executing");

    // raw ctrl-C detaches
    const raw = processInputChunk(Buffer.from([0x03]), r.state);
    expect(raw.actions).toEqual([{ kind: "exit_modal" }]);

    // kitty-encoded ctrl-C detaches identically — this was the freeze
    const kitty = processInputChunk(CTRL_C_KITTY, r.state);
    expect(kitty.actions).toEqual([{ kind: "exit_modal" }]);
    expect(kitty.state.mode).toBe("passthrough");
  });

  it("post-receipts state: kitty Esc and kitty leader both dismiss", () => {
    let r = processInputChunk(LEADER_KITTY, createInputState());
    r = processInputChunk(Buffer.from("status"), r.state);
    r = processInputChunk(ENTER_KITTY, r.state);
    const settled = showReceipts(r.state, ["tail=100"]);
    expect(settled.mode).toBe("modal");

    const viaEsc = processInputChunk(ESC_KITTY, settled);
    expect(viaEsc.actions).toEqual([{ kind: "exit_modal" }]);

    const viaLeader = processInputChunk(LEADER_KITTY, settled);
    expect(viaLeader.actions).toEqual([{ kind: "exit_modal" }]);

    const viaEnter = processInputChunk(ENTER_KITTY, settled);
    expect(viaEnter.actions).toEqual([{ kind: "exit_modal" }]);

Here's the real picture — and it's more consequential than a "new isolated package" merge.

## The headline challenge

Mechanically, the merge is *trivial*: `main` is a direct ancestor of `origin/lhc-convex` (merge-base = `main`'s tip `1b2ee58`), so `git merge origin/lhc-convex` into main is a clean **fast-forward — zero conflicts**.

But **it is not additive.** Those 14 commits sit on top of `3dd0b08 lhc: one-shot work queue — remove retry/backoff` (the `remove-queue-retries` branch). That commit is a **core-SDK behavior change**, not Convex-only:

- rewrites `shared-tech/work-queue/index.ts` (~295 lines gone), `scheduler.ts` (153 lines), `durable-work/index.ts`, `sdk.ts`, `inference-adapter.ts`, `errors.ts` — it rips out retry + exponential backoff entirely
- rewrites **~28 core `lhc` test files** to match one-shot semantics
- already edits `docs/decision-registry.md` and `01-core-concepts.md`, but the rest of the doc corpus (README, 02, 04, the QUEUE-* decisions) still describes retry/backoff — those go stale

So fast-forwarding `lhc-convex` onto main doesn't just add a package — it **changes production behavior for all three shipped hosts** (pi-lhc, cc-lhc, codex-lhc all run on `lhc`'s queue). After the merge, a failed retryable inference call (`rate_limit`, `timeout`, `network`, `empty_output`) lands `failed` on the first try instead of backing off and retrying. For the wrapper hosts that shell out to `claude -p` (which *does* hit rate limits), that's a real regression risk. Plus you'd be landing exploratory WIP (an "example host," live-backend test scaffolding, "51 ported / 1 n/a") onto the trunk.

## Two things that de-risk it

1. **Nothing is at risk of being lost.** The Convex source is already safely committed on `origin/lhc-convex` and local `lhc-convex`. Only *build artifacts* (`dist/`, `.convex/`, `.env.local`) are untracked in this working tree. There's no "rescue the untracked work" urgency.
2. **A fast-forward is locally reversible** (you haven't pushed; `git reset` restores it) — but the *consequence* (retry-removal on trunk) is the part that matters, and whether that's wanted is your call, not mine to assume.

There's also a practical blocker: you're on `cc-lhc-modal-tightening` with the uncommitted modal-freeze work. Moving `main` means dealing with that first (commit it here or stash) — it's unrelated to the Convex work.

Given that "merge all into main" turns out to carry a core behavior change you may or may not intend, I'd rather confirm the direction than silently fast-forward it onto trunk.

# Comprehensive Repository Report: Long Horizon Context (LHC)

## 1. What this is

**Long Horizon Context (LHC)** is a durable context-management system for AI coding agents. The problem it solves: long-running agent sessions produce conversation threads that blow past LLM context windows. LHC records every event of a session to SQLite, derives compressed/summarized forms through an inference pipeline, and serves **"smart compact"** views — intelligently compressed histories that fit a token budget while preserving what the agent needs to keep working.

The architecture is a **single-login-free, in-process SDK** (`lhc`) consumed by **host harnesses** that adapt it to a specific coding agent. It never touches the network itself — all model calls are delegated back to the host through a callback boundary.

**Maturity:** the core SDK is production-shaped and exceptionally disciplined; the hosts are explicitly POC-grade but functional and dogfooded. ~75,000 lines of TypeScript, ~6 weeks of near-daily solo development (Jun 3 – Jul 12 2026), one primary author (Lee Moore, 234 commits), one co-identity ("T3 Code", 22 commits), one external PR contributor.

## 2. Repository layout & tooling

```
long-horizon-context/
├── packages/
│   ├── lhc/         Core SDK — 72 src files / 13.7k lines, 69 test files / 21.2k lines
│   ├── pi-lhc/      PI connector — 50 / 5.9k src, 53 / 9.6k test
│   ├── cc-lhc/      Claude Code PTY wrapper — 30 / 4.9k src, 24 / 5.2k test
│   ├── codex-lhc/   Codex PTY wrapper — 34 / 5.5k src, 29 / 6.2k test
│   ├── t3code-lhc/  Docs-only (4 .md) — design for a web host built in a separate fork
│   └── lhc-convex/  Convex-backend port (just merged to main; see §4/§5)
├── docs/
│   ├── onboard/     01-core-concepts, 02-domain-design, 03-decisions-brief,
│   │                04/05/06-host-{pi,cc,codex}-lhc, bad-code-log
│   ├── decision-registry.md   (1,451 lines — the canonical authority)
│   └── backlog.md   (8 items on the road to v1)
├── vendor/pi/       git submodule → earendil-works/pi (pinned SHA)
├── pi-extensions/   PI harness extensions (exa-search, fast, exit-alias)
├── scripts/ .setup/ Cold-install automation for agents to execute
├── memory/          Agent persistent memory (local-only, untracked)
└── biome.json, pnpm-workspace.yaml, AGENTS.md
```

**Tooling stance:**
- **pnpm workspace**, `pnpm@11.8.0`, **Node ≥ 24.17.0** (deliberately — it depends on the stable built-in `node:sqlite`, which is why the floor is so high).
- **Biome 2.5.0** for format + lint (single tool, no ESLint/Prettier split).
- **Verify gates**: `verify` = format-check → lint → typecheck → fast tests; `verify:all` adds real-inference integration tests (gated behind `LHC_RUN_INTEGRATION=1` + `OPENROUTER_API_KEY`).
- **Vendored PI as a submodule** pointing directly at upstream `earendil-works/pi` at a pinned SHA, with pnpm `overrides` forcing the transitive `pi-ai`/`pi-agent-core`/`pi-tui` deps to the vendored copies. Notably **carries zero local patches now** — see the submodule saga in §4.
- Root `package.json` still calls itself `"liminal-context"` — a **leftover from the pre-rename name** (the repo was renamed to `long-horizon-context`; the `bad-code-log` paths still reference `.../liminal-context`).

## 3. The packages

### 3.1 `lhc` — the core SDK (the crown jewel)

A Node library with **no CLI and no network**. The entire public API is `initLhc(config) → Lhc`, exposing domain namespaces: `threads`, `intakeStream`, `messages`, `turns`, `threadView`, `inspect`, `logging`, `work`.

**Domain model** (each domain owns one part of the conversation model and is reached the same way whether called by the host, another domain, or a future app-server endpoint):

- **Threads** — one durable conversation = one SQLite file (WAL mode). The file *is* the thread; a separate registry maps ids→paths as a lookup convenience, never authority. Thread ids are random (`th_`+hex); everything inside is deterministic positional (`t<n>`, `m<n>`, `c<n>`). Schema-versioned, migrates forward on open.
- **Intake stream** — the *only* content-entry path. Nine closed event kinds, closed schemas (unknown fields rejected structurally via Effect Schema), all-or-nothing batches, thread-scoped idempotency. Turn boundaries are decided **synchronously at intake**, never inferred later; exactly one open turn is a hard invariant.
- **Messages** — normalized readable view (typed blocks + token estimate). Owns two message-level derivations (smoothed prompt, tool-result summary) and edit/delete with cascade invalidation.
- **Turns** — turn state machine + chunk formation. Chunks group whole consecutive closed turns by a deterministic token-arithmetic policy (target 2200 / max 4400). Owns four turn/chunk derivations.
- **Thread view** — smart compact + serving. Four fidelity bands (full live tail → smooth → detailed → brief) as a *gradient, not a cliff*. Compact never calls a model — it's pure assembly from already-derived artifacts, with a fallback ladder (degrade over drop) and a hard refusal on canonical-record damage.
- **Inspect** — read-only reports (overview/health/view), reads through other domains' surfaces, changes nothing.

**Seven derivations** in a dependency hierarchy: 4 inference-backed (`smoothed_prompt`, `tool_result_summary`, `detailed_turn_compression`, `chunk_summary_brief`), 3 deterministic (`turn_rendering`, `pre_detailed_assembly`, `chunk_summary_detailed`). Each carries state (`pending`/`ready`/`failed`/`blocked`) and a `source_version` for optimistic concurrency.

**Shared-tech** (machinery with no domain surface, except logging): a **durable SQLite work queue** with claim/lease + **epoch fencing** + **source-version fencing** + FIFO no-skip-ahead; a **scheduler** (per-thread single-flight drain with pending-flag coalescing); an **inference adapter** (ModelCall → callbacks, input bounding, failure classification, timeout containment); a **prompt registry** (versioned name-keyed templates); an **instance seam** (AsyncLocalStorage scoping so multiple SDKs in one process never cross-drain); local **token counting** (`o200k_base`).

I read `sdk.ts` end-to-end and it is **genuinely excellent code**: the inference-config XOR validation (callbacks *or* config, never both/neither), exhaustive construction-time validation that *throws* on programmer error but returns `OpResult` for operational failure, the `scopeSurface` per-instance seam wrapping, and a clean `registerTestingWork` WeakMap seam that replaced an earlier leak of test hooks onto the runtime surface (documented in the bad-code-log as a fixed smell). The design discipline in the docs is faithfully reflected in the code I sampled.

### 3.2 `pi-lhc` — the PI connector (the reference host)

One package holding a **PI extension** + a **launcher binary**. Every PI message is captured into an LHC thread; PI's native compaction is intercepted and replaced by LHC smart compact; a session is seeded from the LHC thread view instead of a PI session file. The LHC thread is the durable record; the PI session is a rendering of it.

Highlights: an **observe-only hook rail** that never retains PI `ctx`/session objects (they're replaced each call), so `/reload` reconstructs cleanly. Capture is **lagged one hook by design** (PI persists entry ids *after* `message_end`). One LHC turn spans a whole agent run (`agent_end` closes it, not PI's per-step `turn_end`). Capture failures isolate into durable gap `runtime_note`s — nothing throws into PI. The compact bridge is a strict ladder that cancels loudly only on real failure (a 50k floor + genuine errors), with a `seed-entry-map` bridging LHC message ids → PI entry ids across session boundaries. **Largest known debt:** derivation inference rides PI's own model registry (a harness-mediated lane, contamination-adjacent) — the planned fix is the SDK-native inference lane (backlog 17/18).

### 3.3 `cc-lhc` — the Claude Code wrapper (POC)

A **PTY wrapper around the closed `claude` CLI**. Since Claude Code has no extension API rich enough, cc-lhc takes the *outside* position: owns the process, passes the terminal through transparently, and reaches LHC by two side channels — **watching the rollout JSONL** Claude Code writes (capture), and a **ctrl-] leader-key modal** on the alternate screen (control). Because it can't inject context into a running session, compact/prune **rebuild a fresh rollout file + inject `/resume <newId>`** to hot-swap the session in place.

This host contains the most impressive *systems-level terminal engineering* in the repo. The "output doctrine" (the wrapper never writes into a UI it doesn't own), the alt-screen modal that makes input ownership unambiguous by construction, leader recognition across three terminal encodings (raw / kitty CSI-u / xterm modifyOtherKeys), the swap-confirmation oracle (watch for the failure line *and* use rebuilt-rollout growth as ground truth), and failure-safe-by-construction resume — all of it reads as hard-won from live debugging. **This is exactly where your uncommitted WIP lives**: two modal-freeze fixes (kitty-encoded ctrl-C was dropped mid-command leaving no working detach key on kitty terminals; a stray string-terminator introducer wedged the modal) — now captured in `freeze-repro.test.ts`.

### 3.4 `codex-lhc` — the Codex wrapper (POC)

A PTY wrapper around the closed **OpenAI Codex CLI**, built by **copy-adapting cc-lhc** (deliberately not a shared core yet — seam hygiene preserved for a later extraction). Key divergences from cc-lhc: Codex can't hot-swap, so compact/prune **kill-and-respawn** `codex resume <newId>`; Codex stores all sessions in one global date-dir tree, so discovery **requires a cwd filter** (without it, a concurrent Codex session in another workspace hijacks capture — found live); the mapper treats `response_item` as canonical and never intakes the `event_msg` layer (avoids double-writes). Same `claude -p` Sonnet-no-thinking inference lane as cc-lhc.

### 3.5 `t3code-lhc` — docs-only

Four markdown files: design + validation for a **"T3 Code" web host** (capture via provider runtime events, a shared swap controller with a "flip-last cursor invariant", deferred Grok/Cursor research, a Linux deployment handoff). **The implementation lives in a separate `liminal-ai/t3code` fork** — this package is the design record only.

### 3.6 `lhc-convex` — the Convex port (now on main)

A **from-scratch reimplementation of LHC as a Convex component** — the "web/desktop app server" future the core docs hinted at. It mirrors the domain decomposition on Convex's reactive backend (`convex/{schema,intake,queue,threads,view,work,inspect,logging,records}.ts`), plus a `src/client` + `src/shared` logic mirror and an `example/` host with both a fake and a **real OpenAI provider**, exercised against a live local Convex backend. Its `PORTING_LEDGER.md` reports **51 test suites ported / 1 n/a / 0 open**. Critically, it's built on the **`remove-queue-retries` pivot** (`3dd0b08`) — a one-shot work queue with no retry/backoff, because Convex owns scheduling/retry semantics itself. That pivot is a core-SDK change, which is why merging this to main changed trunk behavior for every host (§1 notes).

> ⚠️ The untracked `packages/lhc-convex/` **in your working tree** (on `cc-lhc-modal-tightening`) is still just build artifacts + local backend state + **two `.env.local` files** that likely hold Convex/OpenAI keys. Now that the source is tracked on `main`, those artifacts are gitignored there — but the loose `.env.local` files are worth a look. I did not read them.

## 4. Full git history — the development arc

221 commits, Jun 3 → Jul 12 2026. What's remarkable is the **legibility**: this reads like a textbook of disciplined incremental delivery. The arc breaks into clear phases.

**Phase 0 — Prototype spike (Jun 3–4, 10 commits).** A throwaway vertical slice (`Scaffold lhc thread events core` → `Close chunks with compression artifacts`). Abandoned on Jun 10 when the real build restarts with a formal foundation.

**Phase 1 — Epic-driven core SDK (Jun 10–12).** The signature methodology appears: **numbered Epics, each with a "spec pack" → numbered "Stories" → "fix batches" → a "closeout" with an epic-review pass and a final gate (N/N tests)**. Epic 01 (threads/events/messages/turns/queueing), 02 (derivation pipeline: queue execution, message/turn/chunk derivation, edit+delete cascade), 03 (thread views + smart compact, goldens G1–G4), 04 (read surfaces + inspect), **05 (CLI retirement + inference seam)** — this one is notable for *deleting* an entire CLI surface and 12 spawned-process test suites to prove an SDK-only API, then building the inference adapter + 7 prompts + failure classification. Extraordinary process rigor for a solo (AI-assisted) developer.

**Phase 2 — PI connector, Epic 01 (Jun 14–15).** Extension foundation, session lifecycle, capture, replay verification, derivation recovery.

**Phase 3 — Architecture realignment (Jun 18, "Epic 07").** The big naming/boundary cleanup: derivation-provider seam → **inference callbacks**, `initLhc` introduced, shared-tech public boundary established, Biome added, relic tests + custom gates removed, Node pinned. Commit `0ed3749` here is the reference point cited throughout `bad-code-log.md`.

**Phase 4 — Queue hardening (Jun 19–21).** Epoch fencing on claims, scheduler wake on lease expiry, "harden durable workqueue coordination" — the correctness spine.

**Phase 5 — Inference tuning + comment hygiene (Jun 22–27).** Tool-result classification routing, smooth-turn-compression dial-in, chunk detailed/brief. Plus a striking **~20-commit "Trim stale comments" campaign** in a single day (Jun 22) — comments must state current invariants, not implementation history (a ratified rule, PROC-16). Then legacy-shim removal and smart-compact foundation.

**Phase 6 — cc-lhc host + docs reorg (Jul 1–6).** cc-lhc **slices 1–7** (PTY wrapper → rollout capture → `claude -p` inference → command interception → prune/compact-via-rebuild+resume → cross-launch continuity → own home dir). The v3 compression prompt + "prompt lab." Docs reorg + decisions-brief + **byte-identical resume-fidelity verification**. PI vendored as a submodule.

**Phase 7 — Modal rework + codex-lhc (Jul 6–7).** The fragile "shadow-count MITM" input interception is **thrown out** and replaced by the alt-screen leader-key modal (ambiguity impossible by construction). Then codex-lhc is spun up by copy-adapting cc-lhc, and the modal fixes ported across.

**Phase 8 — Consolidation (Jul 10–12).** `backlog.md` replaces the old `fixes-feature-log` (pruned to 8 items; "git history is the record"), t3code-lhc design docs, **pi-lhc home unification** (`PI_LHC_HOME` → `~/.pi-lhc`, offline migration + backup rail), flags namespaced under `--lhc-*`.

**Phase 9 — the frontier (branches, now partly merged).**
- **`remove-queue-retries` → `lhc-convex`**: the one-shot-queue pivot and the full Convex port (13 further commits, +25k lines). **Just fast-forwarded onto `main` at your request.**
- **Uncommitted WIP**: the cc-lhc modal-freeze fixes on `cc-lhc-modal-tightening`.

**Two sub-sagas worth calling out:**
- **The vendored-PI journey**: fork with a signed-thinking-block patch (Jul 5) → patch merged upstream as PR #6457 (Jul 10) → fork retired, submodule re-pointed straight at upstream carrying *zero* patches (Jul 12). Clean open-source citizenship.
- **The repo rename**: `liminal-context` → `long-horizon-context` (Jul 6), plus public-repo hygiene (untracking `memory/`, sanitizing harvested prompt-lab data and real prompt text from fixtures). The old name still lingers in root `package.json`.

**Contributors:** Lee Moore (234), "T3 Code" (22 — a second commit identity, concentrated in the codex/cc host work), ravensgatedev (1 — external PR #2, "Match Claude Code project path encoding"). Cadence was intense and bursty: peak days of 29 (Jun 22), 21 (Jul 5), 18 (Jun 11) commits.

## 5. Cross-cutting assessment

**What stands out as genuinely strong:**
- **Design-first discipline.** A 1,451-line evidence-cited **decision registry** with statuses and *graveyards* (retired decisions kept as tombstones), a **decisions-brief** orientation cut, per-domain design docs with "last verified against code" stamps and an explicit precedence order (code > README > brief > doc). The docs I cross-checked against source were accurate.
- **A `bad-code-log`** capturing AI-coding failure modes as they were caught (generic `input`/`opts` names, regression-tombstone tests, premature compat shims, queue mechanics leaking onto domain surfaces). This is a rare and valuable practice.
- **Test discipline.** ~1.3–1.5× test:source ratio, deterministic inference callbacks (`fnv1a` digests for stable derivation content without real LLM calls), golden files pinning ratified compact arrangements, explicit "NOT-RAN accounting" instead of silent auth-skips.
- **Correctness machinery** (epoch + source-version fencing, atomic snapshot replace, coverage invariants) reasoned about explicitly.
- **The terminal engineering in the wrapper hosts** is genuinely hard, and it's tackled with an articulated doctrine rather than ad-hoc patches.

**Risks / debt (mostly self-acknowledged):**
- **Trunk now carries the retry/backoff removal** (from the merge you just approved) and the docs describing retries are stale. The three shipped hosts inherit no-retry inference behavior — validate that `claude -p` rate-limit handling is still acceptable, or the retry removal was scoped as intended.
- **Hosts are POC-grade** with open warts carried honestly in their READMEs (janky exit re-emit, ASCII-only modal, lossy rollout rebuild, unbounded wrapper log).
- **pi-lhc inference contamination** (shared PI registry lane) — backlog 17/18.
- **`thread-view` reads other domains' tables directly** for tail assembly — acknowledged boundary debt.
- **Backlog item 35** flagged a dead-duplicate of the version-fenced completion logic in the work queue. I began verifying this against the pre-merge trunk (confirmed `applyDerivationSuccess` in `durable-work` is live; didn't finish checking `work-queue`'s `complete`/`failTerminal` call-sites before the merge detour) — but note the `3dd0b08` queue rewrite that **just landed on main** slashed `work-queue/index.ts` by ~295 lines and likely supersedes this finding. Worth re-checking against the *new* main rather than the code I reviewed.

## 6. Bottom line

This is an unusually mature, thoughtfully-architected system for its age — a clean core SDK with real correctness engineering, wrapped by pragmatic POC hosts, all documented to a standard most teams never reach. The primary open frontier is the **inference lane** (SDK-native HTTP inference to replace host-mediated callbacks) and the **Convex port** you just promoted to trunk. The biggest immediate follow-ups from today: decide on **pushing `main`**, reconcile the **retry-removal doc staleness**, and glance at those loose **`.env.local`** files.
